=== idx: 283 ===
Gold supporting: It has been previously shown that disruption of RAD26 in yeast strain W303-1B results in a strain that is deficient in transcription-coupled repair (TCR), the preferential repair of the transcribed strand of an expressed gene over the non-transcribed strand and the rest of the genome.
LLM supporting:  The provided context does not explicitly mention the specific gene strand targeted by transcription-coupled repair (TCR). However, based on the general understanding of TCR, it primarily targets the non-template (non-coding) strand of DNA. This is because TCR is a mechanism that specifically recognizes and corrects errors in the DNA template strand, which is transcribed into mRNA. The non-template strand, also known as the coding strand, is not directly involved in the transcription process but is used as a reference for the correct sequence of the transcribed mRNA. Therefore, when a mutation or error occurs in the DNA template strand, TCR can identify and correct it, ensuring that the resulting mRNA is accurate. This process is crucial for maintaining the integrity of gene expression and preventing the propagation of errors in the genetic code.

ROUGE-1: 0.3243 ROUGE-2: 0.0765 ROUGE-L: 0.1946

LLM supporting:  The hormone abnormalities characteristic to Pendred syndrome, as described in the context, primarily involve the thyroid gland. In the clinical case provided, the patient with Pendred syndrome exhibited an increase in thyroidal iodine uptake over time, as indicated by a rise in (123)I uptake at 24 hours from 20.2% at age 17 years to 69.4% at age 24 years. This suggests a progressive enhancement in the thyroid's ability to take up and concentrate iodine, which is a hallmark of Pendred syndrome. However, it is important to note that this increased iodine uptake does not lead to elevated TSH levels or impaired iodine organification, as these parameters remained within normal limits. The underlying mechanism for this unique pattern of thyroid function is thought to be related to mutations in the SLC26A4 gene, which encodes the pendrin protein. These mutations, particularly the novel p.T537P and recurrent p.H723R, result in the mislocalization of the pendrin protein, leading to altered iodine transport and potentially stimulating thyroid cell proliferation. This genetic defect results in the characteristic goiter and increased iodine uptake seen in Pendred syndrome, without the typical TSH hyperstimulation or iodine organification defects associated with other thyroid disorders.

The most common adverse event associated with single-agent orteronel is fatigue, while febrile neutropenia is the dose-limiting toxicity when used in combination with docetaxel. Although the ELM-PC5 Phase III clinical trial in patients with advanced-stage prostate cancer who had received prior docetaxel did not achieve the overall survival primary end point, ongoing Phase III trials are still evaluating the efficacy of orteronel in men with earlier stages of prostate cancer.

Therefore, the primary issue in AHDS is the impaired transport of thyroid hormones across the blood-brain barrier, not the concentration of these hormones in the blood.

=== idx: 906 ===
Gold supporting: New case of thyroid dysgenesis and clinical signs of hypothyroidism in Williams syndrome.
LLM supporting:  The case described in the context highlights the presence of hypothyroidism in a patient with Williams syndrome (WS), which is not a common feature but can occur. The patient had elevated thyrotropin (TSH) levels, indicating hypothyroidism, while free T3 and free T4 levels were normal. This suggests a possible pituitary-thyroid axis dysfunction, where the TSH is elevated due to a lack of negative feedback from thyroid hormones, possibly due to the genetic deletion affecting the regulation of the hypothalamic-pituitary-thyroid (HPT) axis. However, it is important to note that hypothyroidism is not a typical or common hormonal abnormality in Williams syndrome. More commonly, the syndrome is characterized by hypercalcemia, which is due to the loss of the ELANE gene, which encodes the elastin protein, leading to an overactive parathyroid hormone (PTH) response. The PTH is increased, leading to hypercalcemia, which is a well-recognized feature of WS. Therefore, while the case presents an interesting and rare occurrence of hypothyroidism in WS, it is not the most common or typical hormonal abnormality associated with the syndrome. The more common and relevant hormonal issue in WS is hypercalcemia, which is related to the genetic deletion and the subsequent overactivity of the PTH.
